- **The automatic "new version" dialog is removed.** It appeared over whatever the user was doing the moment a check found a release. Nobody asked for it at that moment, and an interruption that can only be dismissed teaches people to dismiss it without reading. Checking and installing live in Settings > Software update, reached on the user's own initiative. The background check is kept: it is silent, costs one request, and is what lets that page answer immediately rather than starting from "checking…" each time. `ui/update/UpdateScreens.kt` held nothing but that dialog and is gone.
## [1.2.31] — 2026-09-05

### Added
- **The home screen adapts to the space it has: tablet, television, DeX and a phone turned sideways.** The icon grid was a fixed 4x6 — right for a phone held upright and wrong for everything else, with four columns down the middle and two thirds of the width empty. The shape is now derived, up to six columns, and paged when the icons do not fit so nothing is ever hidden below the bottom edge. An upright phone still gets exactly 4x6, by an early return, because that is the layout every saved icon order was arranged against. Rows are bounded by what FITS and by what is NEEDED: with only the first, a tablet computed six rows for nineteen icons and left the bottom third empty.
- **Android TV is navigable with a remote.** The app declared `LEANBACK_LAUNCHER`, so it installed and appeared in the launcher, and then nothing worked: every control is built on `pointerInput`, which knows nothing about a directional pad, and there was no focus anywhere in the tree. `TvFocus.kt` adds the three pieces that were missing — focusability, a centre-key handler on `KeyUp` (KeyDown repeats while held and opened the same screen several times), and a visible ring. No separate TV layout: the wide board is already the right shape on a television.
- **Settings, the five transport screens and the Cloud screen are two panes on a large display.** List or control on one side, the open page on the other. Right-to-left mirroring comes from `Row` itself rather than from a branch on the language, so Persian puts the list on the right and English on the left with one code path.

### Fixed
- **Sub-pages hid the pane they were opened from.** "Advanced VPN settings" and "Usage" left Settings for a tab of their own, and every transport sub-page replaced the whole screen — so on a wide display the sidebar, or the connect control, disappeared the moment anything was opened. All of them now render in the second column. The Cloud screen's three full pages were early `return`s, which meant the function was over before the account list had been described; they are values now.
- **In landscape the board sat off-centre on the screen.** The display cutout is on one edge and the navigation bar on the other, and the home screen padded only top and bottom, so the icons were centred in the window and visibly off-centre on the glass. The dock also stretched to the full width, putting four icons at the far corners of a very long bar; it is capped and centred now, unchanged on a phone.
- **The wallpaper preview and the preset gallery scaled with their container**, which in a tablet's detail pane produced a preview roughly a foot tall that pushed every control below it off the screen. Both are capped: the preview is a picture of a phone screen, so its useful size is fixed.

### Fixed
- **LAN sharing killed the app on the very first device that connected.** A second device would connect, the phone would show it as connected, and then not one byte moved. An uncaught exception in the local relay: the reverse direction ran in a pooled `Runnable` that had `try/finally` and no `catch`, and closing the socket at the end of every ordinary connection threw there. The exception escaped the Runnable, reached that thread's default handler, and on Android that ends the process — so the app died on the first connection, which is also why no traffic ever moved. Both directions now catch, the close order was changed so a response still arriving is not cut off, and relay threads carry their own `UncaughtExceptionHandler`.
- **Half-open relay connections were never released, and a share could drop mid-download.** When one side half-closed and then said nothing, the socket sat in `FIN_WAIT2` holding two threads for as long as the app lived — measured on a test phone, 12 of the 18 sockets on the relay port. A 90-second grace now closes both sides once one direction has finished. Separately, the relay was synced from a two-second poll, so a single flickering reading (a VPN service reconnecting) dropped every LAN client; three consecutive polls must now agree before it comes down.
- **Proxy Mode was never read for MASQUE, WireGuard or WARP-on-WARP.** The switch only affected the Xray family; `TunnelVpnService` never read `NetworkSettings.proxyMode` at all, and said so in a comment - "VPN mode is the only mode, there is no proxy branch any more". So the switch did nothing for those three, and the Local Network screen told the user to turn on that same switch in order to share - advice that was impossible to follow for exactly the engines it was given for. Turning it on now brings the core up behind `tun2socks` with a local SOCKS listener. The whole device stays tunnelled; there is simply now something another device can connect to. With the switch off the connect path is byte-for-byte what it was.
- **In Proxy Mode no site would open on a tunnel that was healthy.** `tun2socks` reaches its udpgw server from inside the tunnel and, with transparent DNS, every DNS query goes down that path. Psiphon's server intercepts that address; the Rust core has nothing there. Measured on-device: a SOCKS5 CONNECT to `example.com:80` through the core returned `05 00` and fetched the page, while the identical CONNECT to `127.0.0.1:7300` hung until it was killed. TCP was carried flawlessly and no name resolved. `TorSocksFront` — which already implements badvpn's udpgw wire protocol for exactly this reason — is generalised to `SocksFront` and given a second DNS strategy: over TCP, through the upstream, to the configured backend resolver. Tor's own path is unchanged (`dnsPort > 0` still takes the original local-UDP branch, byte for byte).
- **SNI configs appeared in the V2Ray section, inside the BPB folder.** Building one copies the source config and overrides only its address and group, so `engineType` was inherited — and the V2Ray screen builds its tabs from that. They are now filtered out of that screen and appear only on the SNI screen. The filter is applied to the display rather than to the list itself, because several actions on that screen write the whole list back to disk and filtering the source would delete every SNI config the first time a ping was measured.
- **Turning SNI on lit two home lamps.** The test for "this is an SNI session" looked the connected node up in `NodeManager`, whose list loads asynchronously — during the home screen's first composition it was still empty, the answer was "not SNI", and nothing in the cache key changed when the list arrived. `MyVpnService` now publishes the fact it already computes in order to bring the SNI front up.
- **The Cloudflare troubleshooter told token users to fill in their email.** `credentialAdvice()` gated the "a Global API Key needs its email" advice on nothing but a blank email box, so anyone who correctly pasted an API token — which needs no email — was sent after a problem they did not have, in front of the one they did. It is now gated on the probe's own `scheme`, which the screen was already displaying two rows higher.

### Added
- **Crash reports can be sent from the phone in one tap.** Every crash was already written to `files/crashlogs/` with the stack and the trail of screens leading to it, and there was no way for that file to reach anyone — which is the whole problem for a bug several users report and that never reproduces in development. The run after a crash now offers to send it; the report carries the stack, the device model and the app version, and never a config, a credential or any traffic. Reports are grouped on the server by exception type plus the first frame inside the app's own package, so one user hitting the same crash forty times is one row rather than forty.
- **A JSON config can be imported from the clipboard or from a file.** Both importers were line-based and accepted only links, and a JSON config is a single document hundreds of lines long, no line of which is a config on its own — so it always came back "invalid config", even though the app has always been able to connect one (the eight built-in Iran configs are exactly this). The whole text is now read as one JSON document first; a bare object and an array of several are both accepted, and the name comes from `remarks`.
- **The relay speaks HTTP as well as SOCKS5.** The Rust core, Psiphon and Tor publish SOCKS5 only, but the one setting Windows, macOS, iOS and Android all accept is a PAC address and the only entry all of them act on is `PROXY host:port` — Android has no usable SOCKS support in its Wi-Fi settings. Those engines were therefore unshareable in practice. The relay now reads the first byte, passes a SOCKS client through untouched, and answers anything else as HTTP, translating it into a SOCKS5 CONNECT with the hostname left for the far end to resolve.

### Changed
- **The eight built-in free configs are no longer shareable.** The whole share section is hidden for them, QR card included rather than only the copy and send buttons: the QR encodes the same link, so removing the buttons alone would close the two obvious routes and leave the one that needs no tap at all.


### Added
- **Five censorship transports, each with its own home icon and its own screen: MASQUE, WireGuard, WARP-on-WARP, Psiphon and Tor.** They were one icon opening one screen with a tab strip across the top, which made two claims that are both false - that picking one is a mode of a single feature, and that they are variants of each other. They are not. MASQUE and WireGuard reach Cloudflare's edge with completely different handshakes; WARP-on-WARP stacks one inside the other; Psiphon and Tor are separate networks with their own servers, their own countries and their own reasons to fail. A user choosing between them is choosing a different product. MASQUE keeps the dock slot the old combined screen had, because it is the one to try first; the other four lead the icon grid as an unbroken row of four, which is what says they belong together.
  - **Psiphon** runs the real `psiphon-tunnel-core` controller with its 430 embedded server entries, a three-rung protocol ladder ordered by measured time-to-connect on a hostile carrier, an egress-country preference, and Psiphon-over-WARP for the case where the carrier null-routes Psiphon's own addresses. Measured on an Iranian line during development: client region detected as IR, connected on `FRONTED-MEEK-OSSH` (domain-fronted through a CDN, never on a Psiphon-owned IP), exit in the US.
  - **Tor** ships `tor` and `lyrebird` as bundled executables - no Orbot, no companion app - so all three layers of Tor encryption apply to every app on the phone rather than to a browser. Direct, obfs4, meek and Snowflake, plus an automatic ladder that walks them, an exit-country preference over 27 countries with each one's real exit-relay count next to it, and Tor-over-WARP for a network that blocks the Tor network itself.
  - **WARP-on-WARP** is exposed for the first time. It existed in the engine and was hidden behind a flag.
- **Every setting the tunnel core reads is now reachable, including nine that nothing had ever written.** The core has always accepted a log level, a performance profile, in-tunnel DNS servers, per-destination direct and blocked routes, five manual traffic-shaping parameters and a set of Cloudflare Zero Trust credentials; none of them had a control anywhere in the app, so they were fixed at their fallbacks for every user forever. `chain_outer_mode_tor` is the sharpest example: `CoreConfig` had always read it and nothing had ever written it, so Tor's outer leg silently walked the whole ladder however the Psiphon row was set - and the Psiphon row appeared to control something it did not.
- **A connection log screen**, mirrored to a file so it survives the process being killed - which is exactly when it is worth reading.
- **Psiphon's tile carries the Psiphon mark**, seated on the app's own squircle in the red the mark is already painted in, so the disc's edge disappears into the tile instead of reading as a circle in a square hole.

### Changed
- **The transport screens are rebuilt in the app's iOS idiom**, the same one Settings uses: inset grouped cards on the wallpaper, 29pt tinted glyphs, the current value in grey on the trailing edge, and every choice pushing a page of its own rather than opening a dropdown in place. That last part is not styling - it is what lets a row answer "what is this set to" without being opened. The connect control is now the largest thing on the page; the previous screen led with five settings cards stacked above a small button, which put the decisions a user makes once a year above the one they make every day.
- **Psiphon's gomobile runtime is repackaged into the `pg` namespace** (`scripts/repackage-psiphon-aar.py`). It has to be: `libv2ray.aar` and `psiphontunnel.aar` are both gomobile builds, and gomobile gives every library it produces the same `go.Seq` classes and the same `libgojni.so`. Shipping both unmodified is a duplicate-class error and a duplicate-native-library error, and no packaging rule can resolve it - the two `libgojni.so` are different Go programs, so picking one leaves every native method of the other unresolvable at runtime. The rename also has to rebuild the ELF symbol hash table, because `dlsym` finds a symbol by hashing its name rather than by scanning: renaming one in `.dynstr` alone leaves it present and unreachable.

### Fixed
- **None of the five transports counted towards the app's usage figures or the live meter in the home header.** Both were written against the Xray service, so every byte carried by MASQUE, WireGuard, WARP-on-WARP, Psiphon or Tor was invisible to Settings > Total Usage and left the header sitting at zero while the device's whole traffic was going through the tunnel. Both are now fed from the same throttled sample the service already takes, under the same `enable_usage_tracking` switch.
- **The exit country was read from Cloudflare's `loc`, which is the wrong source twice over.** It reports Cloudflare's own view rather than the one every other flag in this app uses — the same disagreement that moved the per-config flags to `api.ip.sb` in 1.2.x — and through Tor it does not report a country at all: every exit reads `T1`, Cloudflare's marker for "this came out of the Tor network", which was being printed on screen as if it were a country. The country is now resolved from the exit ADDRESS through the same geo source as the rest of the app, with two further endpoints behind it so a single one being blocked does not mean no country at all.
- **The exit readout showed a raw IP address and no flag, and named the country in English on a Persian UI.** It is now a flag and a country name in the language the app is set to, built from the platform's region data — so `US` reads "United States" in English and «ایالات متحده» in Persian, with no translation table. The address itself is gone from the screen: it is the input to the answer rather than the answer, and a full IPv6 literal is 39 characters, which is what had been pushing that card out of its own bounds. It is still measured, still logged, and still on the notification. The Tor and Psiphon country pickers and their setting rows were showing the English names baked into the engine packages; they go through the same localisation now.
- **The connect button's halo was animating a layout value.** `Modifier.size(...)` in dp is measured, not drawn, so every frame of that pulse re-measured the halo, the column holding it, and everything below — the status line, the stats card and every settings row breathed in and out for as long as the screen was open. The halo is a fixed size now, and the only motion left is the connecting arc, which is drawn inside a fixed-size canvas and cannot move anything.
- **Content scrolled under the system navigation bar and stopped on a hard line.** The top of every page already dissolved under the status bar; the bottom had simply been missing, so on an edge-to-edge page the last visible row was sliced in half with the navigation buttons floating over it.
- **The exit address and exit country were measured around the tunnel rather than through it.** The service excludes the app from its own tunnel - it must, or the tunnel's traffic re-enters the tun it is feeding - so an ordinary request from the app leaves on the carrier's link however healthy the tunnel is. The probe reported the phone's own address and the phone's own country, and on an Iranian line "the phone's country" and "a tunnel that genuinely exits in Iran" are the same two letters, so the wrong reading was indistinguishable from a true one. It now goes through the transport's own local SOCKS port, which exists on exactly the two transports whose core cannot report its own exit.
- **A connected session displayed the last line of the attempt that had ended.** The detail on a status broadcast describes an attempt in progress, and the CONNECTED broadcast carries none of its own, so a working Psiphon tunnel sat under the words "Psiphon starting..." for as long as it was up. A failure is deliberately still allowed to keep the previous detail, because there it is usually the only statement of what went wrong.
- **The exit country never reached the app at all.** It rides on the status broadcast, and Psiphon names its egress region a second *after* CONNECTED has gone out with no further status due - so the notification knew the country and the screen did not, for the whole session.
- **A tunnel could take the device's network interface away from a running one.** The app now has two `VpnService` implementations, Android grants the interface to one at a time, and the second `establish()` does not fail - it takes it from the first, which carries on believing it still has one. The result is a tunnel whose core is running and whose packets go nowhere, which reads as "the app connected and the internet stopped working". Every start path on both sides now waits for the other to let go.
- **A device the tunnel core was not built for crashed the app on launch** instead of reporting the feature as unavailable: the availability check reached a native method without checking that the library had loaded, from `Application.onCreate`.

## [1.2.2] — 2026-08-30

### Added
- **The home screen is rebuilt as an icon grid, and the hamburger drawer is removed entirely.** Twelve features lived behind that drawer — Fixed IP, Workers, anti-sanction DNS, Sub Link, Usage, Tutorials, About and all three emergency modes — and a feature inside a slide-out menu does not really exist until someone opens the menu. All of them are now icons on a 4-column by 6-row board, alongside Quick Connect, Cloud, Game Boost and MLM Gateway. The four most-used destinations — Scanner, V2Ray, Settings, WireGuard — sit in a glass dock at the bottom, which like an iPhone's is drawn on the home screen only: opening a feature gives it the full screen, and back returns to the grid. The board is six Rows of four weighted cells rather than absolutely positioned children, which is what makes right-to-left free and makes a missing icon impossible: an icon is in a cell or the cell is empty. Tile size is derived from the measured height, because six rows of a fixed 64dp tile plus captions do not fit on a 360x640dp phone.
- **Drag to rearrange, iPhone-style.** Long-press starts a wobble, drag moves the icon, and the order is written to preferences the moment the drag ends. A saved order is reconciled against the destination registry on every load: ids that no longer exist are dropped, and destinations the saved order has never seen are appended in factory order — so shipping a new feature adds an icon to the end of the user's own layout instead of resetting it. Two things make the drag survive: the dragged icon is displaced with `graphicsLayer { translationX }` rather than a layout offset (translation is raw and is never mirrored by layout direction, so a finger moving right moves the icon right in Persian too), and both the drag delta and the wobble angle are read only inside the graphics layer, never in the composition body — read in composition they recomposed all 24 cells on every touch move, and rebuilding a node's modifier chain under an in-flight gesture is what dropped icons mid-drag while the finger was still down.
- **Ten wallpapers plus your own photo, with an adjustable blur,** under a new Settings > Display > Wallpaper. The presets are drawn (a base colour plus soft radial blobs) rather than shipped as images: ten WebP files would have added roughly 3-6 MB to every APK and still been the wrong aspect ratio on some phone, since one crop cannot serve 18:9, 21:9 and a foldable. Custom photos come from the system photo picker, so no storage permission is declared and the app only ever receives the single image chosen; the file is copied into the app's own directory rather than held as a content URI, because a URI grant does not reliably survive a reboot and would leave the home screen with no backdrop. The blur is done by rendering small and letting the upscale smooth it, not by `Modifier.blur` — that is a no-op below API 31, so on anything older than Android 12 the slider would have silently done nothing. The page previews the real renderer at the live blur value, so what you see there is what the home screen shows, and heavier blur costs *less* memory rather than more.

### Changed
- **The grouped cards and the home dock are frosted glass over the real backdrop.** Compose has no backdrop blur and `Modifier.blur` is a no-op below API 31, so this is built the way a compositor would: one small blurred copy of the whole backdrop, `positionInWindow()` to work out which rectangle of it a card is covering, and that slice drawn back at the card's size. The copy is an eighth of each side, so the upscale IS the blur, and one shared bitmap serves every card on screen instead of each allocating its own. Alignment is what sells it -- a few pixels out and it stops reading as glass and starts reading as a texture. Cards fall back to a plain translucent fill when the wallpaper is not behind them, rather than inventing a reflection of something that is not there. Corner radius went from 10dp to 22dp to match.
- **Three new Display settings: icon size, appearance, and whether the wallpaper reaches past the home screen.** Icon size scales the tile only -- captions and cell pitch are deliberately left alone, because scaling the cell would re-space the board and leave the icons the same size -- with a live sample above the slider. Appearance is light/dark/automatic, and reaches the rebuilt surfaces only: the older screens still carry hardcoded dark values, which is why it defaults to Dark rather than Automatic.
- **The app is genuinely edge to edge.** `systemBarsPadding()` is gone from the activity root, so the wallpaper runs under the status bar and the navigation bar with no cut line, on every screen rather than only on the home grid. The inset is applied one level down by AppScreen's feature host, which also fixes a pre-existing fault: eight full-screen overlays (the three emergency screens, the anti-sanction screen, About, Help, Add Node, the GST wizard) already applied `LocalSystemTopPadding` themselves and were therefore being padded twice. Feature screens get a scrim over the wallpaper rather than an opaque sheet, so the backdrop stays present everywhere.
- **Settings, Advanced VPN and Total Usage are rebuilt as iOS-style grouped lists,** and **nothing opens a dropdown in place any more.** Every choice — language, screen-off timeout, backend DNS, local port, MTU, routing mode, the per-app list — pushes a page of its own. That is not styling: it is what frees each row to show its current value in grey on the trailing edge, so "what is this set to" is answered without opening anything. The top of Settings carries the connected Cloudflare account where iOS carries the Apple Account.
- **The Save button is gone from Settings and from Advanced VPN; every change applies immediately.** With a Save button, a screen whose changes had been saved and one whose changes had not looked identical — and Advanced VPN was worse than that: it applied nothing until Save was pressed AND closed itself on save, so backing out silently discarded everything just set. The single exception is the local port, which writes only while the number validates and says why when it does not, because every prefix of a valid port is itself a number.
- **The `enable_aether_tab` and `enable_game_tab` switches are retired.** Both existed only because the old bottom bar could not hold six items; each feature has a permanent icon now. Note that `enable_game_tab` defaulted to off, so Game Boost becomes visible to everyone on upgrade.
- **The app-wide `bottom = 100.dp` nav clearance is swept** from eighteen call sites. It reserved the height of the floating bottom bar, which no longer exists; the pinned Save row in Settings was the most visible case.

### Added (continued)
- **Quick Connect («اتصال سریع»), the app's new landing screen.** A ready-made pool of public servers with a country picker and one-tap connect, on the raised centre button of the bottom bar. It needs no account, no panel deploy and no pasted config, which makes it the shortest path from installing the app to being online. The module is a port of the desktop app's `quick-connect.js`, and carries every part of it:
  - **Two feeds, merged and de-duplicated by real server identity** (protocol+address+port+uuid, not by name). The first is the large public `iampedii/whitedns-sub` list, fetched with a jsDelivr mirror as fallback; the second is the aggregated pool `FreeConfigEngine` already maintains. Two on purpose: either alone is a single point of failure, and one feed being down or filtered still leaves a usable list. Feeds ship either raw links or one base64 blob and both are accepted without being told which. Parsing IS the filter — anything the core cannot speak is dropped before it can reach the tester, because a malformed entry is fatal to the whole generated Xray config rather than to the single node.
  - **A country for every server, derived on-device from the server's own name** — a regional-indicator flag inside the remark, the `DE1`/`NL12` code-token convention, or a keyword table of 60+ countries including their major city names. No network request, so flags are present the moment the list loads rather than only after a delay test. An IP database is deliberately not used: measured across a 2827-server feed, one agreed with the provider's own country label just 34% of the time (Cloudflare anycast, OVH/Scaleway/Oracle ranges are registered in one country and answer from another). Country names come from the platform's region data, so they render in Persian with no translation table, and administrative qualifiers are trimmed so «هنگ‌کنگ» does not arrive four times the width of every other row.
  - **Per-node stable tags.** Feed remarks are eight fields of provider bookkeeping; none of it survives into the list. Each server gets an FNV-1a-derived base36 tag over its endpoint, so a server keeps the same name across refreshes even when the feed reorders itself. The width is 36^4 rather than the naive `% 900`, which on the live feed had 1424 servers sharing a name and a single tag worn by twenty different machines.
  - **A pipelined two-test funnel.** A TCP connect, then a real proxied request through the same primitive the app's own "Real Delay" test uses — the second is necessary because on a filtered line most hosts accept the connection and then carry nothing, so only it proves anything. The two run *concurrently*: 96 TCP probes feed proven-reachable hosts through a bounded channel to 16 real testers, so the first genuine result exists seconds after the press while the reachability sweep is still running behind it. (The first implementation ran the stages in sequence — sweep everything, then test survivors — which on a ~9,000-entry pool meant minutes of nothing followed, usually, by no connection at all. The channel's bound is load-bearing, not incidental: without it the cheap stage races thousands of entries ahead of the expensive one and builds a queue the user is waiting behind.)
  - **The connect button takes the first *good* server, not the first server.** A result at or under 900ms connects immediately; anything worse keeps the sweep running against a 20s deadline and then settles for the best found. The first host to answer is not necessarily one worth using, and the gap between 300ms and 1500ms is the gap between a page loading and a page crawling. When servers are already saved the pool is not touched at all — the saved list is re-tested in parallel and the fastest live entry wins. Re-tested rather than trusted: a stored delay can be hours old and the server may have died since.
  - **A targeted sweep: "N servers from M countries."** Candidates from the M largest-pool countries are interleaved round-robin so every country is being probed from the first second, with a per-country share of ceil(N/M) enforced during collection. A country over its share puts further results aside instead of discarding them, so a country that cannot fill its quota is topped up from the others and a request for 50 comes back with 50 whenever 50 exist. Without the share it would be "N servers" with a filter on top, and one popular country would supply all of them.
  - **Measured countries beat claimed ones (`recordVerified`).** Once traffic has actually gone through a node, `cdn-cgi/trace` is read through the app's own local SOCKS port and reports the country the far end really sees. When that disagrees with the feed's claim the measurement wins, is persisted, and the node moves into its real country for good — it starts appearing when that country is picked and stops appearing under the one it was falsely claiming, with the move announced rather than silently rewritten under the user. A node filed under the wrong flag is not a cosmetic problem; it is the list lying about the one thing it is for. Two guards: a WARP path is reported as verified *transit* only, because WARP returns the USER's country by design and would otherwise file every node under Iran; and readings expire after 30 days, since a year-old verdict about an IP is not evidence about the machine answering on it today. The store is clearable from the country picker.
  - **A stale list beats no list.** The catalog is cached for 30 minutes in memory and on disk; when a refresh fails, the cached list is shown with a notice instead of an error, because the user opened this screen to get online and yesterday's servers can do that while a failed download cannot. The cache carries a version so an upgrade cannot serve rows built by the old code.
  - The panel does not own the connection: the chosen node is handed to `MyVpnService` exactly the way the nodes tab does it, so there is only ever one code path that can say whether the phone is protected.
- **One connect button, with four states that each look different.** The screen leads with a single large button that always means the same thing — press it and you end up online, or offline. With saved servers it takes the fastest; with none it sweeps, finds three, connects to the best and saves the other two so the next press needs no sweep. Idle breathes, searching/connecting sweeps a rotating blue arc with the live stage named underneath, connected is a steady green ring (motion on a settled state is noise, not feedback), and disconnecting sweeps red the other way. Pressing during a search cancels it rather than starting a second one.
- **A persistent "My servers" list under the button.** Servers that have proved themselves survive restarts, sorted fastest-first, with per-row re-test and delete, a header "test all" with a progress bar, and a bulk delete that asks "only the dead ones" or "all". Rows carry a "new" badge until seen and a "down" badge once a test fails. Re-sweeping a country the user has swept before refreshes existing rows rather than duplicating them.
- **A separate "Server list" screen for browsing.** The pool is thousands of entries and mostly dead at any moment, while the connect screen is a short list of proven servers; mixing the two would put a wall of untested rows in front of the one button that is supposed to just work. The browse screen carries the country picker, a "quick check" (stops at 20) and a **"check every server"** that sweeps the whole selected country, per-row and bulk delete, an "already tested"/"new" marker per row so a second sweep shows what it actually added, the source breakdown, and the refresh cadence stated in the header.
- **Deleting a server now means deleting it.** Removed servers are persisted by endpoint identity and dropped while the feeds are being parsed — before they can reach the list, the tester or the cache — so a refresh cannot resurrect them, and a feed republishing the same server under a different name does not either. Identity is the endpoint, not the name. Reversible from the server list screen.
- **VMess and Shadowsocks support, in the app as a whole.** `VpnConfig.parseUri` read only VLESS and Trojan, so every vmess/ss entry in every feed was silently dropped as unusable; `XrayJsonGenerator` now emits both outbound shapes in the main and speed-test configs as well. vmess arrives as a base64 JSON blob whose values are string-or-number depending on the writer; ss has two incompatible spellings in the wild (legacy whole-blob and SIP002) and both are read. ss configs carrying a `plugin=` are deliberately refused — the core starts them and then carries nothing. Because neither link is reconstructable from the parsed fields, `VpnConfig` keeps the original URI and hands it back from `toUriString()` rather than re-spelling it into something the core rejects. The de-duplication key also had to change: ss has no uuid, so its identity is method+password, without which two different ss accounts on one host:port collapsed into one.
- **Four more sources.** Alongside the existing feed, the four `0xRadikal` pools (an aggregator merging ~21 public sources every 15 minutes, pre-filed as verified / fast / secure / all) are now read, best-first so de-duplication files a server under the better label. Measured 2026-08-30: whitedns 410, verified 1494, fast 1079, secure 997, all 10024. **Combined with vmess/ss support this took the usable pool from ~420 unique servers to ~9080** — the earlier number was low almost entirely because the parser was throwing away everything that was not VLESS or Trojan.

### Changed
- **The connection tab's delete button offers "down only" as well as "delete all".** It previously had a single action, so clearing out dead configs meant deleting the working ones with them. The safer action is offered first and carries its own count. What counts as down is deliberately narrow: only a config actually measured whose verdict was `Timeout`, `N/A` or `Error`. `Test` (never measured), `...` (in flight) and `Cancelled` (the user stopped the run) are not verdicts and are never deleted — cancelling a test says nothing about the server. The real-delay result decides when there is one, and ping is consulted only when no delay test has ever run, so a config that answers a ping and then fails the real request is correctly treated as down. Protected Iran defaults are excluded from both actions, and both continue to act only on what is on screen — the open folder, not every folder in the tab.
- **APKs are now built per CPU architecture as well as universally** (see the build note below), so a phone downloads roughly 28 MB less.
- **VPN Gate moved from the centre nav button to the drawer** («گیت‌وی MLM (VPN Gate)»), unchanged in every other respect. The centre button is not just another tab — it is the app's `homeTab`, the first screen after the splash and where back returns to — and a ready-made pool with a country picker is a better answer to "get me online" than a specialist SoftEther surface.

### Fixed
- **Every source reported "failed" with a Compose error as its download error.** Two faults compounding. The catalog download ran in whichever coroutine asked for it, which on the browse screen was composition-scoped, so leaving the screen cancelled it mid-flight. And the per-source handler was a bare `catch (e: Exception)`, which catches `CancellationException` — so instead of the cancellation propagating, each source in turn caught it, recorded "the coroutine scope left the composition" as its own error, and the loop carried on to fail the next one identically. Cancellation is now rethrown everywhere in that path, and the download runs on the repository's own scope with concurrent callers sharing one in-flight job, so no screen can take it down.
- **A freshly connected server could be filed under the wrong country.** The trace ran on a fixed 2.5s delay after the tunnel was asked to start, with no check that the request had actually gone through it. When the core had not finished binding, the reading was the phone's own country — indistinguishable, to that code, from a server that genuinely exits in Iran — and it was recorded as the server's country and shown as its flag. The tracer now takes a baseline of the phone's own public address while disconnected and treats any reading whose IP matches it as not-through-the-tunnel: not trusted, not recorded, and reported to the user as "connected, but the exit country was not verified" rather than as a verified flag. It also retries until the reading is genuinely tunnelled instead of taking one shot at an arbitrary moment.
- **Tapping a different server while connected started a second core on top of the first.** The connect path called `startService` immediately, so the new instance could not bind the local port the old one still held — which the core reports as a proxy error, and which leaves the status probe and the egress trace talking to the *previous* tunnel on that port. Connecting now stops the running tunnel first and waits for the port to be released. This was very likely the same event behind both the English proxy warning seen once on connect and the wrong country that corrected itself on the next reconnect.
- **Leaving the server list cancelled the sweep.** The browse screen ran its sweep in a composition-scoped coroutine, so pressing back mid-run destroyed it and threw away every result it had proven — on "check every server" that is minutes of work lost to looking at another screen. The run now lives in `QuickScanSession`, on a scope of its own, and the screen only observes it: back leaves, the sweep continues, and returning re-attaches with the results so far. Stopping is an explicit request again, not something navigation does silently.
- **The Local Port setting accepted values that quietly broke the app.** The field had no validation whatsoever. Non-numeric text was rescued by the `?: 10808` fallback every reader carried, but a *valid* number in the wrong place was rescued by nothing, and every failure mode was silent: the app also listens on `port + 10000` (a convention the GST engine and the status/country probe share), so `21000` put the probe on `31000` — inside the range the delay tester walks for its throwaway instances — and anything at or above `55536` put the probe past 65535 where it can never bind. A port inside 31000–34999 collided with the tester directly, and 20810 is Aether's. None of this reads as "the port you typed"; it reads as "every server tests as dead". There is now one shared rule (`LocalPort`), the field explains the actual problem and disables Save until it is fixed, the nine separate ad-hoc reads across the app all go through it, and the delay tester is additionally told which ports the live tunnel holds — because a value saved by an older build was never validated at all.
- **A corrected country never reached the connect screen.** The post-connection trace wrote the measured country into the verified store and the in-memory catalog, but the saved list under the connect button is its own store — so a server the trace had just proved exits in Singapore kept showing a Canadian flag in the one list the user is actually looking at. The saved row (flag, country name, and the country prefix in its name) is now rewritten too.
- **Back on the landing screen walked sideways instead of offering to exit.** The global back handler was pinned to a hardcoded `"cloud"` as home while `homeTab` was something else, so back on the landing screen switched to the Cloud tab, and the exit prompt was unreachable from the one screen supposed to own it. It now follows `homeTab`.

## [1.2.1] — 2026-08-09

### Added
- Public GPLv3 source release on GitHub, with a full bilingual README, CONTRIBUTING guide, and Git LFS for native binaries/geo data.
- **Connection tab**: new "Get free configs" button above the `+` add-node button. A multi-step wizard aggregates configs from a few public GitHub-hosted subscription sources, shows how many are available, lets the user pick a target count, then tests them in two stages — a fast TCP reachability pre-check, then a real Xray-proxied connection test (the same primitive the app's own "Real Delay" test uses) — keeping only genuinely working configs. A "this many is enough" button stops the search early and keeps whatever's been found. Results are renamed to random `mlmvpnXXXX` IDs (baked into the URI's own remark so it survives sharing) and land in a "Free Configs" folder under the Manual tab; configs the user already has (matched by real server identity, not name) are skipped and reported instead of duplicated.
- **Connection tab**: real per-config country flags. During the "Real Delay" test, a config that connects and has no cached flag yet gets its exit country queried once through that same live tunnel (via `api.ip.sb/geoip`) and cached on the node — no full VPN connection needed just to see a config's real flag, and it's a one-time lookup afterward served from storage.
- **App updates**: the app now checks GitHub for a newer release right after launch (and again once any VPN connection reaches "connected", in case GitHub itself was blocked at launch). If a newer version exists, a modal shows the new version number, download size, and the full scrollable list of changes, with a "Download new version" button that opens a dedicated download screen (version, size, a fill progress bar with live percentage, then hands off to the system installer automatically once complete). If GitHub is unreachable (no internet, or GitHub itself is filtered) the check fails completely silently — no error, no interruption to anything else in the app.
- **Built-in Iran configs**: two new default configs, **"کانفیگ ایران ۷ (جدید)"** and **"کانفیگ ایران ۸ (جدید)"**, shipped from upstream Serverless-for-Iran **v48** (@patterniha) byte-for-byte untouched. The six existing configs are derived from v44 and stay exactly as they are, so these are additions, not a replacement. v48 is a strategy change, not a parameter tweak: whitelisted geosites (GitHub, Microsoft, OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI, Go/Python/Rust package hosts) now go direct through the system resolver so sanction-blocked services still see an Iranian IP instead of being broken by the evasion path; the DoH resolver itself is domain-fronted through `challenges.cloudflare.com`; TLS traffic gets a dedicated two-stage fragment outbound (splitting the ClientHello's record header, then the body) instead of one blanket fragment rule for all TCP; QUIC and UDP/443 are blocked outright rather than noised, forcing YouTube/TikTok onto the fragmented TCP path; the Iranian filter-page ranges (`10.10.34.0/24`, `2001:4188:2:600::/64`) are blackholed so a hijacked connection fails and retries instead of rendering the block page; and IPv6 is now preferred and raced against IPv4 (`happyEyeballs`). Config #7 vs #8 differ only in the inter-fragment delay pattern — #8 inserts periodic 400 ms pauses for networks whose DPI needs wider gaps. A warning now shows inside the Iran configs folder that these require Local Port `10808`.
- **Connection**: a config's server domain is now resolved by the app *before* the core starts, and the resulting IPs are pinned into the generated config, with the outbound told to dial by IP and race the pinned addresses concurrently (`happyEyeballs`). This matters most for Cloudflare-panel configs (BPB/EDG/Nahan), which all point at a `*.workers.dev` domain that resolves to a large rotating set of Cloudflare edge IPs -- some of which are throttled or dead from Iranian networks at any given moment. Previously the core resolved the domain at dial time and tried the returned addresses one at a time, so a single unlucky IP meant a multi-second stall or an outright connect failure on a config that was otherwise perfectly fine. Resolution happens over DoH addressed by resolver IP (so it needs no DNS bootstrap and isn't at the mercy of an ISP resolver that lies or is slow), falling back to the system resolver; results are cached for 10 minutes. A bare-IP server address skips this entirely, and a total resolution failure just generates the config exactly as before -- it can never block a connection that would otherwise have worked. This lowers connect latency and raises connect success rate; it does not change throughput once connected.
- **Connection**: three wasteful behaviours removed from the app's own generated Xray config, all three found in a live logcat capture from a real device rather than by inspection:
  - **Every DNS lookup cost two round trips.** With no query strategy set, the core asked for an IPv6 (AAAA) record for every hostname, got an empty answer, logged "empty response", and only then asked for the IPv4 record -- measured at 150ms followed by 700ms for a single hostname, repeatedly, for every domain an app touched. The config now follows the device's own address-family preference, so on the IPv4-only mobile networks most users are on the wasted first query disappears.
  - **QUIC was being tunneled to a worker that cannot carry it.** Cloudflare workers carry no UDP, so every QUIC attempt from TikTok/Instagram/YouTube was pushed into the tunnel, stalled, and died with "websocket: close 1005 / 1006 abnormal closure" -- dozens of times in one minute of use in the capture. The app then retried over TCP anyway, so those attempts only ever bought a few hundred milliseconds of delay before each video. QUIC and UDP/443 are now refused immediately so clients fall straight through to TCP. Game and voice traffic on other UDP ports is untouched, and hybrid game configs (whose purpose is to carry UDP out through WARP) are exempt.
  - **Traffic to Iran's block-page ranges was being carried through the tunnel**, paying full proxy latency to fetch a censorship notice. Those ranges are now blackholed so the connection fails fast and the app retries.

- **Real delay test**: the test config is now trimmed to what the measurement actually uses. The delay test starts a fresh Xray core per config (a live capture showed 15 core startups in 6 seconds during one bulk run), and each one was being handed the full connection config -- DNS servers, fake-DNS pool, the whole routing table, a second inbound -- none of which a one-URL measurement touches. The giveaway was that the log never showed a single "listening TCP" line for a test instance, so those inbounds were never even started. Every test instance also used the same hardcoded local port, so concurrent tests were all describing the same two ports; each now takes its own port from a private range clear of the tunnel's and Aether's. Two other paths (the scanner's health test, which was building a full config on port 0, and the Cloudflare IP scanner's per-IP delay test) now use the same trimmed config instead of their own variants. If the trimming ever fails, the full config is used as-is, so a delay test can't fail because of this.
- **Connection**: the WebSocket `Host` value is no longer also written as a `Host` entry inside `headers`. The core logs a deprecation warning for that spelling ("will be removed soon and being migrated to independent host"), and it was the same value in two places, one of them on its way out.
- **Connection**: the core's log level drops from `info` back to `warning`. It was raised to `info` while diagnosing MLM/xhttp lag, and the comment above it said to revert once done. At `info` the core writes several lines per connection through JNI, and a single page load is 50-150 connections, so this was a constant CPU and storage tax on every session for diagnostics nothing reads in a release build. `warning` is also what upstream v2rayNG and the Serverless configs default to.
- **Connection tab**: new **"دامین‌فرانتینگ (بدون سرور)"** folder carrying @patterniha's MITM-DomainFronting v23 profile, which reaches YouTube, Instagram, WhatsApp, Facebook, Reddit and other Fastly-hosted sites with **no server and no Cloudflare worker in the path at all** — nothing to deploy, nothing to pay for, no per-user secret, and no shared bandwidth to saturate no matter how many users run it. The config terminates TLS locally against a certificate the app issues, reads the real destination out of the plaintext, then redials the true server under a different, unblocked SNI. Setup lives entirely inside the folder as a three-step card: the app mints an RSA-2048 root certificate on the device (assembled as DER in-process, since there is no BouncyCastle on the classpath, and written in the exact PEM shapes the bundled core parses — `CERTIFICATE` plus PKCS#1 `RSA PRIVATE KEY`), then hands the certificate to the OS for installation — on Android 10 and below straight into the system installer dialog, and on Android 11+ (where the platform refuses a certificate passed in by an app, answering "this certificate from null must be installed in Settings") by saving it into Downloads under a recognisable name and walking the user through the exact taps in Settings, since no public deep link lands on that screen — then registers the config in the folder ready to select and connect. Trust state is read from `AndroidCAStore` (which holds user-installed CAs and is re-read on every load), matched on the certificate itself so a same-named CA cannot pass, on every resume and on a 1.5s poll while the step is still open — so the step ticks over and the install buttons disappear the moment the OS trusts it, with no app restart and nothing to tap. A collapsible per-brand guide lists the Settings path for Samsung, Xiaomi, Pixel/stock (11 and 12+), Huawei/Honor, Oppo/Realme/OnePlus, Vivo and the rest, plus the search keyword that works on any skin and the Firefox third-party-CA toggle; the local port is forced to `10808` because the upstream config's inbound is hardcoded there and we ship it untouched. **The certificate is generated per device and never leaves it** — a certificate shipped in the APK would hand its private key to everyone who unzips it, letting any user passively decrypt any other user's TLS traffic, which is why upstream warns against sharing one twice in its README. Two limitations are stated plainly in the card rather than buried: the final install confirmation is a system-owned screen that no unrooted app can bypass, and a user-installed CA is honoured by Chromium-based browsers but not by ordinary apps, so this opens the sites in a browser and not in the YouTube or Instagram app.
- **Help center**: new tutorial 17, "Domain Fronting (server-less) — installing and removing the certificate", covering what the method does, the browser-only limitation stated up front, the six setup steps, the security rule about never sharing a certificate, install *and removal* paths for Samsung, Xiaomi/Redmi/Poco, Pixel/stock 11 and 12+, Huawei/Honor, Oppo/Realme/OnePlus, Vivo and the stock-like brands, the "Clear credentials" shortcut with its caveat that it wipes every user certificate, and a troubleshooting list. Eleven new FAQ entries: removing the certificate, why the YouTube/Instagram apps stay blocked, the "from null" install error, the trust step not ticking, whether installing a certificate is dangerous and why you must never install someone else's, Firefox's third-party-CA toggle, Iran configs 7/8 and the 10808 requirement, which mode to pick for which job, why videos used to start slowly (QUIC), and why delay numbers used to read badly. Both languages.
- **Connection tab**: the domain-fronting setup card is now collapsible — tapping its title folds it, and it starts folded once setup is complete so the folder is just the config. It also no longer leaks outside its folder: it was gated on the selected group alone, but with panel grouping switched off the folder row is hidden while the selection is retained, so the card appeared above the flat all-nodes list.
- **Proxy mode**: the app's local port now accepts both SOCKS and HTTP proxy connections (a `mixed` inbound) instead of SOCKS only. An external app that only speaks HTTP proxy can now be pointed at the same Local Port shown in Settings, rather than needing the app's internal secondary port. The secondary port is still opened, because it's a convention shared with the GST engine and the app's own status/country probes target it regardless of which engine is connected.

### Changed
- **Game tab**: removed the two hardcoded legacy UAE WireGuard servers ("Server 1" — a dead 1-hour-trial WireGuard endpoint, "Server 2" — already-retired dead code). Full-tunnel game boosting now goes through the Aether engine, which self-selects a healthy endpoint across multiple protocols instead of a single fixed server.
- **Cloud panel**: bundled BPB Worker Panel upgraded from v4.2.2 to **v5.1.1**. This upstream release changed the deployment model entirely: per-account config (UUID, Trojan password, secure path, account email/token) is now baked directly into the worker's own JS source at upload time (`EMBEDED_SETTINGS`), replacing the old Cloudflare secret-binding approach, which v5 actively rejects. Deploy, panel login (now JSON `{username,password}` instead of a plain-text password, matched against the account email), subscription fetching, and settings save were all rewritten for the new route scheme (routes are now prefixed with a `securePath` obfuscation segment). The in-app settings form was also updated to match v5's schema (a single `protocols` field instead of separate VLESS/Trojan toggles; Proxy IP/NAT64 selection moved to deploy-time only).
- **Cloud panel**: bundled EDG worker (cmliu/edgetunnel) updated to the latest upstream build, carrying several months of upstream transport optimizations (GrainTCP-based up/downlink packet coalescing, TCP concurrent-dial connection racing, DoH resolution caching, China Mobile single-path fallback) that should make EDG configs noticeably faster and more stable to connect. Deploy-time env vars (`UUID`, `PROXYIP`, `ADMIN`, `KV`) are unchanged, so this is a drop-in worker swap; the deploy's `compatibility_date` was bumped to match the new build's requirements.
- **Cloud panel**: bundled Nahan (نهان) worker upgraded from v2.9.4 to **v3.0.0** — upstream VLESS proxy chaining, a v2rayN JSON subscription format, a redesigned add/edit-user form with a modern protocol toggle, and various dashboard RTL/dark-mode fixes. The D1 binding name (`IOT_DB`) and `/api/auth` + `/api/sync` route contract are unchanged, so this is a drop-in worker swap.

### Removed
- **Deno Panel**: removed entirely (drawer entry, tab, FAQ section, and the underlying `engines/deno` deploy/stats code). `api.deno.com` has been blocked in Iran for a while now, and Deno's connection-duration-based billing model made free servers unsuitable for VPN use anyway (constant "Memory Time / Usage Exceeded" suspensions) -- the Cloudflare panels (BPB/EDG/Nahan) cover the same need without either problem.

### Added
- **Connection**: the app now recovers automatically from a real network drop (Wi-Fi/mobile dropping and coming back) while connected. Previously the tunnel had no way to notice its underlying network was gone, so the UI kept reporting "connected" indefinitely -- even after the network came back -- until the user manually disconnected and reconnected. `MyVpnService` now watches real connectivity and, if the network is lost while CONNECTED and later comes back, automatically cycles the same connection (stop, wait for teardown, reconnect) instead of leaving it stuck.

### Changed
- **Aether**: the gateway-scan step (MASQUE) can legitimately take anywhere from tens of seconds to a few minutes depending on scan mode, and on networks that DPI-block MASQUE it runs the full budget before failing -- with only a static "در حال کار" badge, that reads as a frozen/hung app rather than an active (if slow or ultimately unsuccessful) search. The status card now shows a live elapsed-time counter on the scan step, and after 20 seconds an explanatory note that this step can take a while and that some networks block/slow MASQUE, with a reminder that tapping the connect button again cancels it. This does not change scan behavior or success rate -- MASQUE reachability itself is a network/DPI condition outside the app's control -- it only makes a long scan legible instead of looking stuck. (WG and WARP-in-WARP, Aether's other two protocols, stay hidden from this screen; both have known unresolved issues in the current engine build -- gool's inner tunnel gets dropped by the same edge as the outer one and never carries traffic, and WG's watchdog reconnects endlessly without passing data -- so surfacing them would trade one confusing failure for another.)

### Fixed
- **Status bar**: on phones with the system set to light mode, the status bar's clock/battery icons could render dark on top of the app's own always-dark background, making them invisible (worked fine only if the phone itself was already in dark mode). The app's UI never actually follows the system light/dark setting -- its color scheme is hardcoded dark -- but edge-to-edge was letting the status/nav bar icon color follow that system setting anyway instead of what the app actually draws. Icon color is now forced to always match the app's own background, regardless of the phone's system theme.
- **Scanner**: real delay/speed test results (an actual Xray-proxied connection per config, not a raw ping) could read far worse than the network actually was -- hundreds of configs tested with up to 8 real proxied connections running concurrently against the same servers, which reads as suspicious concurrent-handshake traffic to Cloudflare/DPI and gets throttled. The resulting delay/speed numbers were measuring self-inflicted congestion from the test itself, not real conditions. One of the two concurrency limits involved even carried a comment ("Cloudflare/DPI might block if >3 concurrent handshakes") directly contradicting the `8` it was actually set to. Both are now capped at 3, matching the safe value already used elsewhere in the app for the same kind of test.
- **Battery**: substantially reduced battery drain while connected. Four separate causes, all of which cost power continuously for as long as the VPN was up:
  - The tunnel held an **unbounded partial wake lock** for the entire session, so the CPU could never enter deep sleep or Doze while connected — including overnight with the phone idle in a pocket and not a single byte moving. The lock is now held only while traffic is actually flowing and released after 5 minutes of silence; incoming packets still wake the process through the tunnel interface regardless, and the lock is re-taken on the next tick that sees movement. It also now carries a timeout, so an abnormally-killed service can no longer strand it held.
  - A leftover **diagnostic loop woke every 5 seconds** for the whole session purely to write a RAM figure to the log that nothing reads in a release build — roughly 17,000 wakeups a day doing no work. Removed entirely.
  - The traffic meter **wrote to storage every 2 seconds** whether or not anything had changed — about 43,000 pointless disk writes a day on an idle tunnel. It now only writes when bytes actually moved.
  - The auto-switch watcher **re-read a single boolean every 10 seconds** even though the feature is off by default, so for most users that was ~8,600 needless wakeups a day to learn nothing. Now checked once a minute (the feature's own interval is 15+ minutes, so this costs nothing).
- **Subscription links**: the background auto-sync re-uploaded a link's configs to Cloudflare on *every* node-list change, and most of those changes are metadata-only (a ping result, a speed-test figure, a country flag) that leave the actual config list byte-identical. Every one still cost a full Cloudflare API round trip, all day, for every link on every account — wasted battery and data, and enough API traffic to plausibly contribute to the Cloudflare errors some users saw after leaving the app running for a day. Auto-sync now skips the upload entirely when the config list hasn't actually changed.
- **Cloud panel**: adding a Cloudflare account could get permanently stuck behind a "verify your email" overlay with no way out except an external Cloudflare login, even when email verification wasn't actually the problem. Root cause: when creating the account's `workers.dev` subdomain failed, the app decided it must be an unverified email purely by checking whether Cloudflare's raw error text happened to contain the word "verify" -- which several unrelated Cloudflare errors (token scope issues, rate limiting, etc.) can also contain, incorrectly locking the whole cloud panel behind the verification screen with no visibility into the real cause. It now asks Cloudflare's own account-status endpoint for the real verified state before showing that screen, and surfaces the actual error otherwise.
- **Subscription links**: a sub-link's configs could silently go empty on their own after a while, well before the user ever pressed "Update" -- v2rayNG (or any other client) would refresh the link and get zero configs back, even though the link had worked fine right after creation. Root cause: sub-links store which node group they serve as a composite string (`"manual:<engine>:<groupTitle>"`, `"cloud:<groupId>:<engine>"`, etc.), and the screen's own "Update" button correctly parses that string apart to find the matching nodes -- but the background auto-sync job (which re-uploads a link's configs a few seconds after any node list change, e.g. a ping/speed-test result) was comparing a node's plain engine type directly against that whole composite string, which can never match. So every background sync silently overwrote the link with an empty config list, and the "after 20-30 refreshes" the user saw was really however long it took one of those background syncs to fire after the link was last set correctly by hand. The auto-sync job now parses the group reference the same way the Update button does.
- **Node connection**: connecting a V2Ray/VLESS node in "Proxy mode" (local SOCKS/HTTP proxy only, no full-device VPN tunnel) could leave the app showing "connected" while the local proxy passed no traffic at all -- every site timed out through it, even though the app's own state looked healthy. Root cause: proxy mode deliberately skips creating the VPN tunnel interface (so there's no real file descriptor for it), but the Xray config was still being built as if a tunnel existed, handing xray-core a "tun" inbound pointing at a bogus/absent descriptor. xray-core accepted this at startup (reporting success), then the invalid inbound could crash the whole engine loop a few seconds later -- taking the SOCKS/HTTP proxy down with it, silently, after the UI had already committed to "connected". The tunnel inbound is now only included when a real VPN interface actually exists.
- **Navigation**: the bottom nav bar's icons looked lopsided whenever the optional Game/WireGuard tabs made the two sides hold a different number of icons (e.g. 3 left / 2 right) -- the shorter side's icons visibly clustered toward the centre button with a dead gap past them, instead of being evenly spread, and the icons on that side ended up a different size than the other side's. Both sides now use a fixed, identical per-icon width and centre their (unpadded) icon group within their half, so icons stay the same size and evenly spaced no matter which optional tabs are on.
- **Navigation**: the hamburger drawer's menu items left a dead empty gap between the last item and the bottom of the screen on tall phones, instead of using that space. The layout now expands a gap between the main menu and the emergency section on tall screens (pinning the emergency section to the bottom) while still scrolling normally with everything packed together on short screens where it doesn't all fit.
- **Cloud panel**: adding a Cloudflare account with a Global API Key and no email showed a vague "Invalid API Token" error. It now explains clearly that Global API Keys require an email, with a hint shown under the field.
- **Cloud panel**: MLM worker deploys failed intermittently with "Failed to upload MLM worker". Root cause: the worker's `compatibility_date` was computed from the device's local clock instead of a fixed date; for timezones ahead of UTC (e.g. Iran, UTC+3:30), roughly between midnight and 03:30 local time the computed date landed a day ahead of Cloudflare's UTC clock and the upload was rejected. Now pinned to a fixed, safe date, matching every other worker deployer in the app.
- **Cloud panel**: MLM (and Nahan) deploys could get permanently stuck failing at "Failed to create D1 Database" after a few retries, with no way to recover short of manually deleting old databases in the Cloudflare dashboard. Root cause: every deploy attempt -- including retries of a failed one -- provisioned a brand-new D1 database and never reused or cleaned up earlier ones, so a handful of retries would quietly eat into the account's D1 database quota until none was left to create. Deploys now reuse the account's existing database (or an orphaned one from a previous attempt) instead of always creating a new one. If the account's 10-database Free-plan limit is genuinely full with databases from other tools/projects, the error now lists every existing database's name so you know exactly what to delete, instead of a generic "Failed to create D1 Database."
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: the domain checker's "استعلام" (check) button only classifies a domain -- it never added it to the routing list on its own, but its "sanctioned" message read as if turning the feature on would be enough. Users would check a domain, see it's sanctioned, turn the feature on, and be confused when the site still didn't open because they never pressed the separate "افزودن" (add) button. The message now says to press "افزودن", and if a domain comes back sanctioned but isn't in your list yet, a red warning makes that explicit.
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: sanctioned sites (confirmed "sanctioned" by the feature's own domain checker) still didn't open, showing 403s exactly as if the feature were off. Root cause, found via a live logcat capture from a real device: domain routing relied entirely on sniffing the SNI out of the TLS ClientHello -- which silently fails for any site using Encrypted Client Hello (ECH, increasingly Chrome's default), since the SNI is encrypted and invisible to sniffing. Every "sanctioned" connection was falling through to the catch-all direct rule and hitting the origin from the real Iranian IP. Fixed by resolving sanctioned domains through a reserved fake-IP DNS pool instead of relying on sniffing -- A second live logcat capture showed the fakedns resolution itself was now working correctly (sanctioned lookups were getting IPs from the configured fake pool), but routing was still sending that traffic `direct` -- the fake-IP-to-domain reverse lookup wasn't reliably matching the domain-based routing rule on this Xray build. Routing sanctioned traffic now matches directly on the fakedns pool's IP range (`198.18.0.0/15`) instead of depending on that reverse lookup at all, since anything resolved into that range can only be a sanctioned domain to begin with.
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: turning this feature on could break sites that were never even supposed to be touched (e.g. Gmail), because its DNS resolver was hardcoded to plain UDP `1.1.1.1:53` -- a protocol commonly blocked or throttled on Iranian ISPs -- with no fallback, unlike the main VPN config which already resolves via DoH over HTTPS for exactly this reason. It now resolves via the same DoH-over-443 approach, so DNS keeps working (and non-sanctioned domains keep resolving normally) even when plain DNS is blocked.
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: added an "Update worker" button once a worker is already deployed, so you can redeploy the latest bundled EDG worker onto the same account without deleting and re-adding it.
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: after successfully deploying the exit worker, the screen kept showing the "build worker" button instead of switching to "ready" -- the user had to leave and re-enter the screen to see it had actually worked. Root cause: the deploy functions update the Cloudflare account object's fields in place, so the list pushed into the shared account state afterward was structurally identical to what was already there (same mutated object references), and Kotlin's `StateFlow` skips re-emitting a value it considers unchanged -- so this screen never recomposed. Now tracks deploy success locally instead of waiting on that round-trip.
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS**: the "build worker" step (same EDG worker deploy used elsewhere) could fail permanently on repeated retries for the same reason MLM/Nahan did -- it always created a brand-new KV namespace and never reused or cleaned up earlier ones. It now reuses the account's existing KV namespace (or an orphaned one from a previous attempt), and deploy failures now show the real Cloudflare error instead of a generic message.
- **Scanner tab**: the "Ping Health Test" button on a combined/mixed config group (and the MLM "Update subscription with healthy IPs" flow that depends on it) could hang indefinitely or crash the app on some devices. Root cause: this specific code path was missing the native Xray core initialization call that every other testing feature in the app performs first (it was commented out) -- whether it happened to work depended entirely on some other screen having already initialized the core earlier in the same app session, which is why it worked reliably for some users/flows and not others. Restored the missing call.
- **Cloud panel**: the MLM "User Management" screen could get permanently stuck on "Error fetching users" right after a fresh MLM deploy. Root cause: Cloudflare's own edge returns a transient `404` (its own error code `1042`) for the first few seconds after a brand-new `*.workers.dev` route is enabled, before the worker code even runs -- the app now retries a few times automatically before treating it as a real error, and any real failure now shows the actual HTTP status/response instead of a generic message.
- **Cloud panel**: BPB worker uploads failed with Cloudflare error 10021 ("No such module node:crypto") — v5.1.1 requires the `nodejs_compat` compatibility flag, which wasn't being sent.
- **Cloud panel**: BPB-generated configs connected to nothing (empty SNI/Host, falling back to BPB's own placeholder domain `www.speedtest.net`) because the worker's `mainDomain` was left blank; it's now set to the deployed worker's actual `*.workers.dev` hostname.
- **Navigation**: the hamburger drawer menu could get cut off on short screens, making the emergency-tier options at the bottom unreachable. Drawer content now scrolls fully.
- **Connection tab**: the country flag shown above the Connect button came from Cloudflare's own geoIP database (the `loc` field in `cdn-cgi/trace`), which occasionally disagreed with reality (e.g. showed Canada for a US exit IP). It now uses the same `api.ip.sb/geoip` source as the per-config flags, so both agree with each other and with third-party checkers like ip.me.

---

## [1.2.0] — 2026-07

### Added
- **GATE MLMVPN** — a new connection engine reachable over TCP :443 (works on lines that throttle UDP), with a large connect button in the bottom nav, thousands of servers with country flags, and a persistent archive of every server ever seen (not just currently-live ones).
- Real connection testing, separate from ping: a full handshake against the server, not just RTT — green means it actually connects on your line.
- Browse servers by continent/country, multi-select, batch test, add healthy ones to your main list, and clear the unresponsive ones.
- Optional UDP acceleration toggle for networks where UDP is open.
- New app-launch animation.

### Changed
- Full redesign of the WireGuard tab: large central connect button, key settings directly beneath it, secondary settings on their own screen, connection status at the bottom.

### Fixed
- App used to crash and relaunch a few seconds after a Google Apps Script disconnect — the tunnel's native core was pulling the whole app down with it on shutdown. It now runs isolated from the app process.
- WireGuard: a healthy tunnel used to be misdetected as dead after ~12s idle and reconnected from scratch; the idle-vs-dead detection window was fixed.
- WireGuard: automatic fallback to another transport if the first doesn't respond, and automatic re-keying if the WARP identity is rejected.

### Security
- Server IP:port is no longer shown on server cards (still sortable by those fields) to avoid leaking it in screenshots.

---

## [1.1.0] — 2026-06

### Added
- **Personal anti-sanction DNS** — routes only sanctioned domains (ChatGPT, Gemini, GitHub, Steam, ...) through your own Cloudflare Worker with a clean IP; everything else stays direct. Any domain can be looked up and added.
- Full redesign of the second-tier emergency flow (Google Apps Script): step-by-step wizard (choose password → confirm → get ready-to-deploy code with a guide and direct link → enter Deployment ID → test the relay). Multiple Google accounts supported for load spreading.
- CA certificate for the second-tier emergency flow can now be generated and installed directly from the top card, no prior connection required.

### Fixed
- Copied Apps Script code is now clean (no BOM, no stray comments); added a "save as file" option for devices whose clipboard truncates large copies.
- App used to hard-close to the launcher on second-tier emergency disconnect; it now refreshes itself in a controlled way.
- Back-button behavior standardized: returns to the Cloud (home) tab from anywhere, steps back one level in two-step screens, and asks for confirmation to exit from the home tab.

---

## [1.0.9] — Emergency-conditions release

Optimized specifically for degraded/censored network conditions (server creation, management, and connection kept working under heavy filtering).

- Second-tier emergency (Google Apps Script) fully optimized for stability under bad networks.
- First-tier emergency (Vercel) now covers all 4 panels (MLM, Nahan, BPB, Edge): deploy, settings, user management, and config retrieval all work over Vercel even when direct Cloudflare access is blocked.
- Fixed xHTTP cloud-config lag and closed a DNS leak in xHTTP configs (queries now resolve encrypted, inside the tunnel).
- Six protected, non-removable default Iran configs, each with a different bypass strategy for different carriers.

## [1.0.8]

- Dedicated UAE WireGuard server for lower game ping, usable across the whole Game tab server list.
- Full Game tab redesign; AUTO mode now compares every ping-reduction method by real measured ping.
- Cloudflare Dedicated DNS with country-based smart steering.
- Free, unlimited dedicated UAE DNS service as a lightweight full-tunnel-free ping-reduction option.

## [1.0.8-beta]

- New Deno panel: fully automatic dedicated-server provisioning with no credit card, multi-account support, and per-account usage stats.
- Deno VLESS/Trojan configs over both WebSocket and xHTTP.
- Idle-connection auto-close and smarter in-tunnel request routing for free Deno server longevity.
- Quick-Settings tile: toggle the VPN and auto-connect to the fastest of your recent servers with one tap.
- Significant app size reduction (dead code removed, code shrinking enabled) while staying universal across device ABIs.
- Dedicated Game Booster system: automatically picks direct vs. tunnel routing for minimal ping.

## [1.0.7]

- New serverless connection method for degraded-network conditions, with 5 ready-made default servers (bypasses censorship, not usable for Telegram since it uses an Iranian IP).
- New dedicated MLMVPN panel with stable XHTTP configs.
- Rewritten scanner: more accurate delay/ping/speed tests.
- Fixed false ping readings shown with no actual internet connection.

## [1.0.6]

- New subscription-link (sub-link) generation system.

## [1.0.51]

- Full Android 15/16 compatibility fix: menu items no longer overlap the navigation bar.

## [1.0.5]

- SNI anti-filter engine with 400+ bundled configs.
- First-tier (Vercel Tunnel) and second-tier (Google Script Tunnel) emergency systems introduced.
- Core rewrite of all three cloud panels (NHN, BPB, EDG).
- Progress bars for ping/delay/speed tests.
- Manual MTU tuning.
- Unified worker management across a Cloudflare account (view/delete individually or in bulk).
- Per-platform config testing (YouTube, Instagram, + 8 more).
- Automatic background server failover, configurable per-app.
- English + Persian localization with automatic OS-language detection.
- New app icon and broad UI/UX overhaul.
- Optional WARP tab hide/disable.

<a id="فارسی"></a>
## فارسی

نسخه‌بندی این فایل مطابق `versionName` در [`app/build.gradle`](app/build.gradle) است. برای جزئیات کامل‌تر و به‌روزتر هر نسخه، داخل خود اپ به «درباره ما → لیست تغییرات» مراجعه کنید.

### [1.2.39] — 2026-10-01

**افزوده‌شده:**
- **کاشی تازهٔ «آمنزیا».**
  - سرورهای رایگان وایرگارد با مبهم‌سازی آمنزیا، لینک‌های wireguard و هیستریا ۲ از ۱۱ مخزن گیت‌هاب (با آینهٔ jsDelivr وقتی گیت‌هاب فیلتر است).
  - فایل conf/zip خود کاربر، چسباندن از کلیپ‌بورد و «باز کردن با» از تلگرام.
  - ۱۳۵ سرور سالم داخل خود برنامه است، پس بدون دانلود اولیه هم کار می‌کند.
  - دیلی واقعی و سریع مثل v2ray، در پروسه‌ای جدا تا کرش یک سرور برنامه را نبندد. یادگیری جداگانه برای هر اینترنت، حذف خودکار سرورهای مرده و بروزرسانی منابع.
  - دسته‌بندی کشوری؛ بعد از تست یا اتصال، سرور به کشور واقعی خودش منتقل می‌شود. سرورهای وارپ دستهٔ جدا دارند.
  - دکمهٔ اتصال تا اتصال واقعی در حالت لودینگ می‌ماند و هر لحظه قابل لغو است.
  - ظاهر iOS: صفحهٔ اصلی با دکمهٔ اتصال و «مکان سرور»، و صفحهٔ جدای سرورها با جستجو و فیلتر کشور. حذف با کشیدن فقط با کشیدن کامل انجام می‌شود و قابل بازگردانی است.
  - برای فایل وایرگارد ناقص، دقیقاً گفته می‌شود چه چیزی کم است.
- **«دکتر کلادفلر» در ابزارها و عیب‌یابی حساب.** در یک اجرا چهار چیز را جدا بررسی می‌کند و می‌گوید هرکدام کجا خراب می‌شود: حساب کلادفلر، دریافت کانفیگ از پنل‌ها، اتصال کانفیگ‌ها، و این‌که چرا اسکنر آی‌پی چیزی پیدا نمی‌کند.
